Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A rename that also moves a notification

A fictional author and reviewer resolve an unintended side effect in a pull request.

  1. Reviewer: The helper rename is clear, but moving the notification changes the failed-export path.
  2. Author: I intended only a rename. Which movement changes what the user would see?
  3. Reviewer: The call now sits outside the success branch, so failure would also produce a completion notification.
  4. Author: You're right. That's an unintended side effect, not part of the requested cleanup.
  5. Reviewer: Could you retain the success condition and add a check for the failure path?
  6. Author: Yes. I'll restore that condition and assert that failed exports don't show completion.
  7. Reviewer: The naming choice isn't a blocker. My required change concerns the notification's behavior.
  8. Author: That distinction helps. I'll leave the name and focus the revision on the conditional call.
  9. Reviewer: Once revised, I'll review the changed branch and the new assertion together.
  10. Author: I've accepted that request. This remains a rename with preserved behavior, not a notification redesign.
An optional suggestion among required changes

Fictional reviewers distinguish a readability preference from an incorrect boundary check.

  1. Author: You left comments about the variable name and the upper-bound check. Are both blockers?
  2. Reviewer: The boundary check is required: this contract accepts ten, but the new comparison rejects it.
  3. Author: And the name suggestion? I used the same name as the neighboring helper.
  4. Reviewer: That's optional. I find limit clearer, but the existing naming convention is defensible.
  5. Author: I'll correct the boundary and keep the established name to avoid unrelated changes.
  6. Reviewer: That works. Please include the accepted value ten and the rejected value eleven in the evidence.
  7. Author: Those examples make the intended boundary clear without expanding this into a style cleanup.
  8. Reviewer: Exactly. My review should distinguish a behavior mismatch from a preference about readability.
  9. Author: I'll mark the naming thread as discussed and return the boundary revision for review.
  10. Reviewer: Then the required action is unambiguous, and the optional comment doesn't become an accidental condition.
A helper extracts more behavior than expected

Fictional developers discuss a refactor that unexpectedly reorders an observable event.

  1. Reviewer: The extracted helper sends the receipt before the save completes. Was that ordering change intentional?
  2. Author: No. I moved both operations into one helper but changed their order accidentally.
  3. Reviewer: The existing behavior sends only after a successful save. Callers may rely on that distinction.
  4. Author: Refactoring should preserve that observable sequence, not merely reuse the same calls.
  5. Reviewer: Could you keep the original ordering and explain the helper's responsibility more narrowly?
  6. Author: Yes. It will coordinate the existing sequence rather than introduce a new notification policy.
  7. Reviewer: Please keep the unrelated retry proposal outside this pull request so we can review each behavior separately.
  8. Author: I'll separate that proposal. The current revision will only extract the unchanged sequence.
  9. Reviewer: That gives the tests a clear expectation: no receipt before the successful save outcome.
  10. Author: And it makes the refactor claim something we can actually check against observable behavior.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A rename that changed the failure path
  1. Read the review once, keeping acceptance of the name separate from the blocking behavior concern.
  2. Swap roles for turns 5-10. Stress success-only notification and no notification after failure.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Turn a vague objection into a review request

Complete the review summary while preserving the accepted rename and unresolved behavior issue.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reviewer: "The helper ___ is acceptable."

Answer and explanation

A. name

Priya explicitly accepts the naming change in the pull request.

2. Author: "The notification must remain on the ___ path."

Answer and explanation

C. success

Completion notification is intended only for a successful export here.

3. Reviewer: "Add a test for the ___ path."

Answer and explanation

B. failure

The regression occurs when export fails yet a completion notice appears.

4. Author: "The current feedback is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. approval

The blocking behavior issue still requires correction and verification.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Giving actionable code-review feedback
Original fictional case: A pull request is titled Rename export helper. The diff also moves the completion-notification call outside the success branch. Failed exports therefore notify users as though export completed. Reviewer Priya accepts the naming change but raises the behavior change with author Tomas. The review should request restoration of success-only notification and a failure-path test. The acceptable rename does not justify approving the unintended notification behavior.
Language workshop: A rename that changed the failure path
Communication goal: Anchor a review comment in a changed call location, its observable effect, and a specific correction and test.
Language guidance: A rename can be acceptable while the same diff contains a blocking defect. Moving the notification outside the success branch changes observable behavior, so it is not merely a naming preference or a behavior-preserving refactor.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The helper name is clear. / This notification call has moved outside the success branch. / A failed export can now send a completion notice. / My concern is behavior, not the naming choice. / Was this control-flow change intentional? / The completion notice belongs on the success path. / Please restore the success-only condition. / Add a test for a failed export. / The failure case should not send a completion notification. / This change introduces a false-success path. / I cannot approve this behavior yet. / I am not asking you to undo the rename. / The diff does more than the title suggests. / Please update the patch and its description. / I will review the revised behavior and test. / The rename can stay once the notification issue is resolved.
Vocabulary: pull request: Proposed code change submitted for review; abbreviated PR. / diff: Display of changes between versions. / review comment: Feedback attached to a proposed change. / success branch: Execution path entered when the relevant operation succeeds. / failure path: Execution path taken when an operation fails. / call site: Location where a function or operation is invoked. / control flow: Order and conditions under which code executes. / side effect: Observable effect beyond a function's returned value. / completion notification: Message indicating an operation has finished successfully here. / behavior change: Alteration in what the system observably does. / refactor: Internal restructuring intended to preserve observable behavior. / rename: Change to an identifier's name. / regression test: Test guarding against recurrence of a known defect. / assertion: Test statement checking an expected condition. / blocking concern: Issue that must be resolved before approval under the review process. / nonblocking suggestion: Improvement that does not prevent approval under the process. / review scope: Set of changes and concerns covered by a review. / intent: Purpose the author means a change to achieve. / observable behavior: Effect visible to a user or another component. / false success: Indication of success when the operation did not succeed. / guard condition: Condition controlling whether an action may occur. / patch revision: Updated version of a proposed change. / approval: Explicit acceptance at the relevant review stage. / verification evidence: Results supporting a claim that a change behaves as intended.
Speaking task: Read the review once, keeping acceptance of the name separate from the blocking behavior concern. Swap roles for turns 5-10. Stress success-only notification and no notification after failure. Repeat the final four turns. Include the regression check and pending review rather than announcing approval.
Partner: Priya: Code reviewer / Tomas: Pull-request author
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the review summary while preserving the accepted rename and unresolved behavior issue.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: Make sure the new check would fail if that notification moved outside the success condition again. Otherwise it won't protect this behavior.
Tomas: I'll verify the regression test catches that specific recurrence. Simply executing the failure branch isn't enough if the assertion misses the notice.
Priya: Good. My review remains blocked on corrected behavior and its check. I'm happy with the name; those are separate decisions.
Tomas: I'll return with verification evidence for the correction. We can resolve the blocking comment after you've reviewed that, not from this conversation alone.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Requesting dependency evidenceA direct upgrade with an indirect change+

Your goal

Request the resolved dependency version, affected call sites, and migration evidence before claiming upgrade compatibility.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A direct upgrade with an indirect change

A fictional application uses ParcelKit directly. ParcelKit in turn uses TextCore. The proposed ParcelKit upgrade changes the resolved TextCore version. A change note marks a parsing entry point as deprecated. Developers Leila and Ben have not established whether the application or its dependencies use that entry point, whether it has been removed, or whether the replacement is compatible. The packages are not assumed to follow any particular versioning convention.

Leila: Developer proposing the upgrade / Ben: Developer checking dependency impact

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which dependency does the application use directly?

Answer and explanation

B. ParcelKit

The application uses ParcelKit directly, while TextCore is introduced through ParcelKit.

2. What changes indirectly?

Answer and explanation

C. The resolved TextCore version

The proposed ParcelKit upgrade changes the TextCore version selected in the dependency resolution.

3. What does the note establish?

Answer and explanation

A. A parsing entry point is deprecated

Deprecation is stated, but usage, removal, and replacement compatibility remain unverified.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Direct describes the application-to-ParcelKit relationship. Transitive describes the application-to-TextCore relationship through ParcelKit. The dependency actually selected matters, not just the version written in the top-level upgrade request.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
direct dependency
Package explicitly used or declared by the application here.upgrade a direct dependency
transitive dependency
Package brought in through another dependency.inspect a transitive dependency
dependency tree
Structure showing how packages depend on one another.inspect the dependency tree
resolved version
Specific version selected after dependency resolution.record the resolved version
lockfile
File recording resolved dependency selections for reproducibility.inspect the lockfile
version constraint
Rule limiting which dependency versions may be selected.check a version constraint
release note
Published description of changes in a release.read the release note
deprecation
Notice that an interface is discouraged or scheduled for replacement.investigate a deprecation
removal
Elimination of a previously available interface or behavior.verify removal separately
migration guide
Instructions describing transition to a changed interface.consult the migration guide
entry point
Named interface through which functionality is invoked.locate the parsing entry point
call site
Location that invokes a particular interface.search for affected call sites
public interface
Supported surface exposed for other components to use.compare the public interface
breaking change
Change that can invalidate previously supported use.identify a breaking change
compatibility
Ability to work with the relevant existing consumers and behavior.verify compatibility
semantic versioning
Version convention relating version numbers to public-interface changes.confirm semantic versioning policy
major version
Leading version component under a stated convention.interpret the major version
minor version
Version component whose meaning depends on the adopted convention.check minor-version guarantees
patch version
Version component commonly used for fixes under a stated convention.verify patch-version policy
pin
Fixed dependency selection restricting version movement.review the version pin
changelog
Maintained record of notable changes between versions.compare the changelog
replacement interface
Interface intended to supersede another.assess the replacement interface
resolution
Process selecting concrete dependency versions.reproduce dependency resolution
upgrade evidence
Records and checks supporting an upgrade assessment.assemble upgrade evidence

Deprecated does not itself mean removed, unused, or compatible with a replacement. Version numbers provide guarantees only under the package policy that actually applies. Request usage and migration evidence before declaring the upgrade safe.

C Notice the language

Ask what changed below the headline

An upgrade can affect more than the package named in the request. Asking for the resolved dependency tree and affected calls is a practical evidence request, not resistance to maintenance. Avoid translating deprecated into removed or compatible without checking the actual contract.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the direct dependency
The application uses ParcelKit directly.
Identify the indirect dependency
TextCore comes through ParcelKit.
Name the additional change
The resolved TextCore version also changes.
Request the actual selection
Which version does the lockfile record?
Ask for the relationship
Can you show the dependency tree?
Quote the narrow finding
The note marks the parsing entry point as deprecated.
Avoid an unsupported upgrade
Deprecated does not by itself mean removed.
Ask about usage
Do we have any affected call sites?
Include indirect callers
Check whether ParcelKit invokes it as well.
Request migration detail
What does the migration guide say about the replacement?
Separate availability and behavior
An available replacement is not automatically compatible.
Qualify the version inference
We need the package's actual versioning policy.
Request verification
Which checks exercise the affected parsing behavior?
Keep the decision open
We do not have enough evidence to claim compatibility.
State the pending work
Usage and replacement behavior still need review.
Close the request
Please attach the resolved versions and the relevant findings.
6 language and precision notes
Comes through
Expresses an indirect dependency relationship in ordinary speech.
Also changes
Exposes a second effect hidden by the top-level upgrade description.
By itself
Limits what the deprecation label establishes.
Whether
Leaves actual usage unresolved.
Not automatically
Challenges a shortcut inference without claiming the opposite.
Actual
Directs attention to the selected versions and applicable policy.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. TextCore is transitive through ParcelKit

TextCore is brought into the application through its direct dependency ParcelKit.

2. What should be checked first about the selected version?

Answer and explanation

B. The resolved dependency record

The resolved record establishes the concrete TextCore version selected by this upgrade.

3. What does deprecated not establish?

Answer and explanation

B. That the entry point has been removed

Deprecation and removal are different statuses, and removal has not been established.

4. Which compatibility claim is justified now?

Answer and explanation

D. Compatibility remains unverified

Usage and replacement behavior have not yet been checked.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

A direct upgrade with an indirect change

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leila: Developer proposing the upgrade / Ben: Developer checking dependency impact

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • call site
  • semantic versioning
  • dependency tree
  • migration guide
  • compatibility
  • lockfile
  • deprecation
  • transitive dependency
  • upgrade evidence
  • public interface
  1. Leila
    The ParcelKit upgrade also selects a different TextCore version. The request title doesn't show that indirect change, but the resolved list does.
  2. Ben
    Then we need to review the 1 change as well. The application uses ParcelKit directly, but that does not make TextCore irrelevant to its behavior.
  3. Leila
    I can attach the package constraints and actual selected versions. Which record do you need to see what this build will really use?
  4. Ben
    Please include the 2 and the relevant dependency relationship. A constraint can allow several versions, while the resolved record tells us which one this change actually selects.
  5. Leila
    The release notes mark a parsing entry point deprecated. I don't yet know whether our code or ParcelKit's internals call it.
  6. Ben
    Keep that as a 3 finding, not a removal finding. The note deserves investigation, but its label alone does not tell us whether this application is affected.
  7. Leila
    I'll remove my claim that the upgrade deletes a parser we use. Deprecation, removal, and our usage are three different questions.
  8. Ben
    Correct. Start by locating any relevant 4, including indirect calls through ParcelKit. An absence of direct application calls would not automatically rule out an indirect dependency on that behavior.
  9. Leila
    The replacement has a similar name. Before I switch anything, where should we check whether its inputs and error behavior actually match?
  10. Ben
    Consult the 5 first. Similar names do not establish identical inputs, outputs, error behavior, or handling of edge cases. We need the relevant documented differences.
  11. Leila
    The version looks familiar, but we haven't checked either package's versioning policy. I shouldn't infer its compatibility promise from the number alone.
  12. Ben
    Then do not assume 6 guarantees. We can check the actual policy, but a familiar number pattern is not evidence that its promised compatibility rules apply.
  13. Leila
    I'll trace ParcelKit to TextCore in the review notes. That should explain why a package we don't call directly still matters to this upgrade.
  14. Ben
    Good. The 7 will help explain why TextCore belongs in the discussion even though the request is named after ParcelKit.
  15. Leila
    The existing suite passes, but I haven't shown that it exercises the affected parsing entry point. What evidence should the review request?
  16. Ben
    Ask which checks exercise the relevant 8. A passing suite cannot establish coverage of a path it never executes.
  17. Leila
    I'll name that coverage gap instead of saying all tests passed therefore compatible. A green suite could simply miss the changed path.
  18. Ben
    Exactly. We need 9 tied to this change. It should show what was checked and what remains unknown, not just that an upgrade command succeeded.
  19. Leila
    My revised summary will separate resolved versions, deprecation status, usage, and replacement behavior. The last two still need investigation.
  20. Ben
    That is accurate. Keep 10 unverified for now. Once those findings are available, we can discuss the upgrade on evidence rather than infer its impact from the headline.

Answers and explanations
  1. transitive dependency Turn 2. TextCore is a transitive dependency because the application receives it through ParcelKit rather than using it directly here.
  2. lockfile Turn 4. The lockfile records resolved dependency selections and is more specific than a top-level version constraint alone.
  3. deprecation Turn 6. Deprecation marks an interface for discouragement or transition but does not alone establish removal or current usage.
  4. call site Turn 8. A call site shows where the affected parsing interface is invoked, which must be checked rather than assumed.
  5. migration guide Turn 10. The migration guide provides transition details that a similar replacement name cannot establish.
  6. semantic versioning Turn 12. Semantic versioning provides specific conventions only when the relevant project actually adopts and follows that policy.
  7. dependency tree Turn 14. The dependency tree makes the ParcelKit-to-TextCore relationship visible so indirect impact is included in the review.
  8. public interface Turn 16. The public interface is the exposed parsing surface whose supported behavior and affected callers need verification.
  9. upgrade evidence Turn 18. Upgrade evidence combines actual versions, affected usage, documentation, and relevant checks rather than a broad assertion of safety.
  10. compatibility Turn 20. Compatibility remains unverified until the relevant consumers and behavior have been checked against the proposed versions.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leila: The ParcelKit upgrade also selects a different TextCore version. The request title doesn't show that indirect change, but the resolved list does.
  2. Ben: Then we need to review the transitive dependency change as well. The application uses ParcelKit directly, but that does not make TextCore irrelevant to its behavior.
  3. Leila: I can attach the package constraints and actual selected versions. Which record do you need to see what this build will really use?
  4. Ben: Please include the lockfile and the relevant dependency relationship. A constraint can allow several versions, while the resolved record tells us which one this change actually selects.
  5. Leila: The release notes mark a parsing entry point deprecated. I don't yet know whether our code or ParcelKit's internals call it.
  6. Ben: Keep that as a deprecation finding, not a removal finding. The note deserves investigation, but its label alone does not tell us whether this application is affected.
  7. Leila: I'll remove my claim that the upgrade deletes a parser we use. Deprecation, removal, and our usage are three different questions.
  8. Ben: Correct. Start by locating any relevant call site, including indirect calls through ParcelKit. An absence of direct application calls would not automatically rule out an indirect dependency on that behavior.
  9. Leila: The replacement has a similar name. Before I switch anything, where should we check whether its inputs and error behavior actually match?
  10. Ben: Consult the migration guide first. Similar names do not establish identical inputs, outputs, error behavior, or handling of edge cases. We need the relevant documented differences.
  11. Leila: The version looks familiar, but we haven't checked either package's versioning policy. I shouldn't infer its compatibility promise from the number alone.
  12. Ben: Then do not assume semantic versioning guarantees. We can check the actual policy, but a familiar number pattern is not evidence that its promised compatibility rules apply.
  13. Leila: I'll trace ParcelKit to TextCore in the review notes. That should explain why a package we don't call directly still matters to this upgrade.
  14. Ben: Good. The dependency tree will help explain why TextCore belongs in the discussion even though the request is named after ParcelKit.
  15. Leila: The existing suite passes, but I haven't shown that it exercises the affected parsing entry point. What evidence should the review request?
  16. Ben: Ask which checks exercise the relevant public interface. A passing suite cannot establish coverage of a path it never executes.
  17. Leila: I'll name that coverage gap instead of saying all tests passed therefore compatible. A green suite could simply miss the changed path.
  18. Ben: Exactly. We need upgrade evidence tied to this change. It should show what was checked and what remains unknown, not just that an upgrade command succeeded.
  19. Leila: My revised summary will separate resolved versions, deprecation status, usage, and replacement behavior. The last two still need investigation.
  20. Ben: That is accurate. Keep compatibility unverified for now. Once those findings are available, we can discuss the upgrade on evidence rather than infer its impact from the headline.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The package we didn't list directly

Fictional developers identify evidence needed for a ParcelKit upgrade review.

  1. Reviewer: ParcelKit is our direct dependency; what introduces TextCore into the proposed build?
  2. Maintainer: TextCore is a transitive dependency through ParcelKit. Its resolved version changes in the proposed lockfile.
  3. Reviewer: Could you show that difference and link the note about the deprecated parsing entry point?
  4. Maintainer: I have both. I haven't checked whether our application or ParcelKit uses that entry point.
  5. Reviewer: Please identify the caller before describing this as an update requiring no code changes.
  6. Maintainer: Agreed. Deprecation doesn't automatically mean removal, but the actual call and version still matter.
  7. Reviewer: Exactly. We need evidence about this combination, not an assumption based on the package name.
  8. Maintainer: I'll return the dependency path, resolved versions, change note, and relevant call-site findings.
  9. Reviewer: That will let us separate package resolution from compatibility with the calls we make.
  10. Maintainer: And we'll leave the drop-in claim unconfirmed until those findings support it.
A lockfile difference without a source change

Fictional maintainers discuss why two builds resolved different dependency sets.

  1. Developer: My source changes only a label, yet the proposed build resolves three different package versions.
  2. Maintainer: Did the dependency manifest change, or only the lockfile that records this resolution?
  3. Developer: Only the lockfile differs. I don't know which tool action regenerated it locally.
  4. Maintainer: Then the label change and dependency change need separate explanations before we call the build equivalent.
  5. Developer: Could I describe the lockfile as generated and therefore irrelevant to behavior?
  6. Maintainer: No. Generated doesn't mean inconsequential; the resolved dependencies may affect what goes into the artifact.
  7. Developer: I'll identify how it changed and compare the selected versions with the current build.
  8. Maintainer: Please check the actual tool documentation rather than assume every package manager treats lockfiles identically.
  9. Developer: Understood. The source edit remains narrow, but the artifact difference is not yet explained.
  10. Maintainer: That's the right scope for review: one known label change and an open resolution difference.
Deprecated is not removed

A fictional maintainer clarifies an upgrade note with a caller's developer.

  1. Caller developer: The note says the old reader is deprecated. Does our current call stop working immediately?
  2. Maintainer: The note doesn't establish removal. It says the replacement is preferred, with removal timing unspecified.
  3. Caller developer: So we should distinguish a migration recommendation from a confirmed missing entry point.
  4. Maintainer: Yes. We also need to compare the replacement's input and error behavior before changing callers.
  5. Caller developer: Our current caller expects an error result for malformed input. Does the replacement preserve that?
  6. Maintainer: The note links a contract section, but I haven't checked that specific behavior yet.
  7. Caller developer: Please include it in the review. A matching function name wouldn't settle that difference.
  8. Maintainer: I'll compare the stated contracts and identify any behavior our caller relies on.
  9. Caller developer: Then today's conclusion is deprecated use identified, replacement compatibility still under review.
  10. Maintainer: Exactly. We can discuss a migration without inventing an immediate failure or a removal deadline.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 04: Requesting dependency evidence
Original fictional case: A fictional application uses ParcelKit directly. ParcelKit in turn uses TextCore. The proposed ParcelKit upgrade changes the resolved TextCore version. A change note marks a parsing entry point as deprecated. Developers Leila and Ben have not established whether the application or its dependencies use that entry point, whether it has been removed, or whether the replacement is compatible. The packages are not assumed to follow any particular versioning convention.
Language workshop: A direct upgrade with an indirect change
Communication goal: Request the resolved dependency version, affected call sites, and migration evidence before claiming upgrade compatibility.
Language guidance: Direct describes the application-to-ParcelKit relationship. Transitive describes the application-to-TextCore relationship through ParcelKit. The dependency actually selected matters, not just the version written in the top-level upgrade request.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The application uses ParcelKit directly. / TextCore comes through ParcelKit. / The resolved TextCore version also changes. / Which version does the lockfile record? / Can you show the dependency tree? / The note marks the parsing entry point as deprecated. / Deprecated does not by itself mean removed. / Do we have any affected call sites? / Check whether ParcelKit invokes it as well. / What does the migration guide say about the replacement? / An available replacement is not automatically compatible. / We need the package's actual versioning policy. / Which checks exercise the affected parsing behavior? / We do not have enough evidence to claim compatibility. / Usage and replacement behavior still need review. / Please attach the resolved versions and the relevant findings.
Vocabulary: direct dependency: Package explicitly used or declared by the application here. / transitive dependency: Package brought in through another dependency. / dependency tree: Structure showing how packages depend on one another. / resolved version: Specific version selected after dependency resolution. / lockfile: File recording resolved dependency selections for reproducibility. / version constraint: Rule limiting which dependency versions may be selected. / release note: Published description of changes in a release. / deprecation: Notice that an interface is discouraged or scheduled for replacement. / removal: Elimination of a previously available interface or behavior. / migration guide: Instructions describing transition to a changed interface. / entry point: Named interface through which functionality is invoked. / call site: Location that invokes a particular interface. / public interface: Supported surface exposed for other components to use. / breaking change: Change that can invalidate previously supported use. / compatibility: Ability to work with the relevant existing consumers and behavior. / semantic versioning: Version convention relating version numbers to public-interface changes. / major version: Leading version component under a stated convention. / minor version: Version component whose meaning depends on the adopted convention. / patch version: Version component commonly used for fixes under a stated convention. / pin: Fixed dependency selection restricting version movement. / changelog: Maintained record of notable changes between versions. / replacement interface: Interface intended to supersede another. / resolution: Process selecting concrete dependency versions. / upgrade evidence: Records and checks supporting an upgrade assessment.
Speaking task: Read the dependency discussion and distinguish the requested ParcelKit change from the selected TextCore version. Swap roles and repeat turns 5-12. Keep deprecated, removed, used, and compatible separate. Check the final readback against turns 17-20. Usage and compatibility must remain unresolved until evidence is supplied.
Partner: Leila: Developer proposing the upgrade / Ben: Developer checking dependency impact
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the upgrade handoff without turning deprecation into confirmed removal or compatibility.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leila: I'll name that coverage gap instead of saying all tests passed therefore compatible. A green suite could simply miss the changed path.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Explaining what a test actually exercisesA green test that never sees rejection+

Your goal

Explain the limits of a success-only test double and distinguish a failure-path unit test from real-adapter integration coverage.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A green test that never sees rejection

A save-operation unit test uses a storage double that always succeeds and checks the success message. A previous bug showed success after storage rejected the save. The current test never produces rejection, and the real storage adapter is untested. Developers Owen and Farah need to configure a rejection case, assert that false success does not appear, and keep real-adapter integration coverage as a separate unresolved concern.

Owen: Developer maintaining the test / Farah: Developer reviewing coverage

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the storage double currently do?

Answer and explanation

A. Always succeeds

The supplied test double is configured only for successful storage.

2. What did the previous bug show?

Answer and explanation

C. Success after storage rejected the save

The prior defect was a false success indication after rejection.

3. What remains separately untested?

Answer and explanation

C. The real storage adapter

The scenario explicitly states that the real adapter has not been tested.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A double that always succeeds can support a success-path assertion, but it cannot exercise rejection handling. Configure a failing response and check the user-visible consequence relevant to the known bug.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
unit test
Focused test of a small behavior or component.extend the unit test
test double
Substitute for a real dependency during testing.configure the test double
stub
Test substitute supplying predetermined responses.use a success stub
mock
Test substitute that can record or check interactions.inspect mock interactions
fixture
Prepared state or data used by a test.arrange the fixture
success response
Result indicating an operation succeeded.return a success response
rejection
Failed outcome reported by the storage operation here.simulate a rejection
exception
Error signal raised during execution under the relevant model.configure an exception
assertion
Explicit check of an expected test condition.add a negative assertion
negative assertion
Check that an unwanted event or state does not occur.verify a negative assertion
test isolation
Separation of a tested unit from unrelated dependencies.preserve test isolation
integration test
Test of interaction between connected components.add an integration test
adapter
Component translating between an application and an external interface.test the storage adapter
branch coverage
Measure of which decision outcomes a test set executes.examine branch coverage
happy path
Expected successful execution path.cover the happy path
error path
Execution path handling an unsuccessful operation.exercise the error path
false negative
Test result that misses a defect which is present.avoid a false negative
test oracle
Rule or reference used to judge the expected result.define the test oracle
arrange
Prepare the conditions for a test.arrange a rejection case
act
Execute the behavior being tested.act on the save request
assert
Check the observed result against the expectation.assert no false success
interaction verification
Check of how a component called its dependencies.separate interaction verification
behavioral verification
Check of the externally observable result.perform behavioral verification
coverage gap
Relevant behavior not exercised by the current tests.report the coverage gap

The rejection unit test can verify application behavior against a simulated storage outcome. It does not establish that the real adapter produces that outcome correctly or that the actual integration behaves the same way.

C Notice the language

A passing result needs a scope statement

Green is a useful status, but it is not a complete explanation. Name the behavior the test actually exercises and the dependencies it replaces. This allows a colleague to request missing coverage without denying the value of existing tests.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Describe the current setup
The storage double always succeeds.
Name the tested path
This test exercises the happy path.
Recall the defect
The bug showed success after a rejected save.
Identify the gap
The current test never triggers rejection.
Request a configured outcome
Make the double reject in a separate case.
Specify the check
Assert that no success message appears after rejection.
Preserve existing coverage
Keep the successful-save case too.
Separate call and result
A storage call alone does not prove the right user message.
Name the substitute
This is a double, not the real adapter.
Limit the conclusion
The result applies to the simulated rejection case.
Request another test level
The real adapter needs separate integration coverage.
Avoid a blanket claim
Green tests do not establish all storage behavior.
State the open question
We have not verified the real adapter.
Ask about expectations
What observable result should this case check?
Request evidence
Please include the configured outcome and assertion.
Close accurately
The new unit case can close one gap, not every storage risk.
6 language and precision notes
Always
Exposes a fixture that cannot produce the missing failure condition.
After rejection
Connects the assertion to the relevant event sequence.
Separate case
Preserves success coverage while adding failure coverage.
Alone
Limits what a dependency-call assertion can demonstrate.
Simulated
Marks the boundary between a double and real integration.
One gap
Avoids overstating the scope of the proposed improvement.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Why does the current test miss the prior bug?

Answer and explanation

C. Its double never rejects

The failing condition required to expose the bug is absent from the fixture.

2. Which addition targets the defect?

Answer and explanation

C. Configure rejection and assert no success message

The new case must trigger rejection and verify the missing user-visible boundary.

3. What does the rejection unit test not establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Correct behavior of the real storage adapter

The test still substitutes for the real adapter rather than exercising it.

4. Which report is precise?

Answer and explanation

B. Success-path coverage exists; rejection and real-adapter coverage need work

The report separates existing coverage from two distinct unresolved areas.

Answers and explanations
  1. happy path Turn 2. The happy path is the successful execution route supplied by the always-successful storage double.
  2. test double Turn 4. The test double replaces real storage and must be configured to produce the rejection needed by this test.
  3. negative assertion Turn 6. A negative assertion checks that the unwanted success message does not appear after the simulated rejection.
  4. Interaction verification Turn 8. Interaction verification checks dependency calls but does not alone establish the correct user-visible result.
  5. integration test Turn 10. An integration test would exercise the relevant connected components, whereas this case still replaces the real storage adapter.
  6. coverage gap Turn 12. The coverage gap for the real adapter remains open even if the simulated rejection unit case passes.
  7. arrange Turn 14. Arrange is the preparation stage that configures the rejection condition before the save behavior is executed.
  8. success response Turn 16. Success response is the outcome configured in the existing test, so its evidence is limited to that path.
  9. behavioral verification Turn 18. Behavioral verification checks the observed application result, while retaining the boundary imposed by the substituted dependency.
  10. unit test Turn 20. A unit test can cover the application's rejection handling without proving the behavior of the real storage integration.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The storage substitute never rejects

Fictional developers examine why a unit test missed a misleading success message.

  1. Reviewer: The save test passed before the bug report. What outcome did its storage substitute return?
  2. Developer: It always returned success, and the assertion checked that the success message appeared.
  3. Reviewer: Then it never exercised the rejection that previously produced the wrong message, did it?
  4. Developer: No. We need a regression case where the substitute rejects and the visible result is checked.
  5. Reviewer: Would asserting only that the function returns without throwing catch the misleading success message?
  6. Developer: Not necessarily. The assertion needs to distinguish the intended rejection message from false success.
  7. Reviewer: Good. And what would that case tell us about the real storage adapter?
  8. Developer: Only about the save function's response to the simulated outcome, not the adapter's actual behavior.
  9. Reviewer: Then name the integration question separately so the unit test isn't credited with answering it.
  10. Developer: The unit check covers rejection response; an integration test must examine the real adapter.
A test that calls code but checks no result

Fictional developers strengthen the stated purpose of a parsing test.

  1. Developer: This new test calls the parser with a valid record and completes without an exception.
  2. Reviewer: What behavior is it intended to establish? Execution alone doesn't check the parsed values.
  3. Developer: It should preserve leading zeros in a reference, but I haven't asserted that result.
  4. Reviewer: Then a parser returning a different reference might pass this test as currently described.
  5. Developer: You're right. The concrete input is zero zero seven, and the expected reference retains all three characters.
  6. Reviewer: Please make that output the check, rather than relying on successful execution as a substitute.
  7. Developer: That also explains why converting the reference to a number would violate this contract.
  8. Reviewer: Exactly. The test's purpose is preserving an identifier, not checking arithmetic on its digits.
  9. Developer: I'll add the expected reference and name the case around preserving leading zeros.
  10. Reviewer: Then a failure will point to the behavior we care about instead of an unspecified parser problem.
A test double with the wrong contract

Fictional developers find a mismatch between a controlled substitute and a real collaborator.

  1. Reviewer: The test double returns a plain string, but the collaborator's contract returns a result object.
  2. Developer: I simplified it because the function only needs the label inside that object.
  3. Reviewer: Does the function actually extract the label, or does the simplified substitute hide that step?
  4. Developer: It extracts the label in production. The test currently bypasses that shape difference.
  5. Reviewer: Then the substitute is not exercising the interface the function is supposed to consume.
  6. Developer: I'll make its returned shape match the contract and keep the sample label simple.
  7. Reviewer: Please include the error result shape too if this case claims to cover rejection.
  8. Developer: This case covers success only. I'll label it narrowly and add rejection as a separate case.
  9. Reviewer: That separates test data simplicity from changing the collaborator's promised interface.
  10. Developer: Agreed. A controlled substitute can be predictable without teaching the test the wrong contract.

Lesson 05: Explaining what a test actually exercises
Original fictional case: A save-operation unit test uses a storage double that always succeeds and checks the success message. A previous bug showed success after storage rejected the save. The current test never produces rejection, and the real storage adapter is untested. Developers Owen and Farah need to configure a rejection case, assert that false success does not appear, and keep real-adapter integration coverage as a separate unresolved concern.
Language workshop: A green test that never sees rejection
Communication goal: Explain the limits of a success-only test double and distinguish a failure-path unit test from real-adapter integration coverage.
Language guidance: A double that always succeeds can support a success-path assertion, but it cannot exercise rejection handling. Configure a failing response and check the user-visible consequence relevant to the known bug.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The storage double always succeeds. / This test exercises the happy path. / The bug showed success after a rejected save. / The current test never triggers rejection. / Make the double reject in a separate case. / Assert that no success message appears after rejection. / Keep the successful-save case too. / A storage call alone does not prove the right user message. / This is a double, not the real adapter. / The result applies to the simulated rejection case. / The real adapter needs separate integration coverage. / Green tests do not establish all storage behavior. / We have not verified the real adapter. / What observable result should this case check? / Please include the configured outcome and assertion. / The new unit case can close one gap, not every storage risk.
Vocabulary: unit test: Focused test of a small behavior or component. / test double: Substitute for a real dependency during testing. / stub: Test substitute supplying predetermined responses. / mock: Test substitute that can record or check interactions. / fixture: Prepared state or data used by a test. / success response: Result indicating an operation succeeded. / rejection: Failed outcome reported by the storage operation here. / exception: Error signal raised during execution under the relevant model. / assertion: Explicit check of an expected test condition. / negative assertion: Check that an unwanted event or state does not occur. / test isolation: Separation of a tested unit from unrelated dependencies. / integration test: Test of interaction between connected components. / adapter: Component translating between an application and an external interface. / branch coverage: Measure of which decision outcomes a test set executes. / happy path: Expected successful execution path. / error path: Execution path handling an unsuccessful operation. / false negative: Test result that misses a defect which is present. / test oracle: Rule or reference used to judge the expected result. / arrange: Prepare the conditions for a test. / act: Execute the behavior being tested. / assert: Check the observed result against the expectation. / interaction verification: Check of how a component called its dependencies. / behavioral verification: Check of the externally observable result. / coverage gap: Relevant behavior not exercised by the current tests.
Speaking task: Read the save-test exchange, stressing always succeeds when describing the original double. Swap roles and repeat turns 5-12. Distinguish a no-success-message assertion from checking a dependency call. Use the final four turns to rehearse a bounded test report. Include the simulated rejection and remaining real-adapter gap.
Partner: Owen: Developer maintaining the test / Farah: Developer reviewing coverage
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the review note about the current test and the proposed rejection case.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Owen: After adding rejection, I'll report exactly which outcome was simulated and which visible behavior was checked, alongside the real-adapter gap.
Farah: That is useful behavioral verification. Report the assertion result, not a blanket statement that storage is fully tested.
Owen: The patch is still pending review. I'll attach both test results when they're available, without inventing a successful run in the handoff.
Farah: Good. A targeted unit test can catch this application defect while leaving the real integration question clearly open.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Original fictional case: A save-operation unit test uses a storage double that always succeeds and checks the success message. A previous bug showed success after storage rejected the save. The current test never produces rejection, and the real storage adapter is untested. Developers Owen and Farah need to configure a rejection case, assert that false success does not appear, and keep real-adapter integration coverage as a separate unresolved concern.
Language workshop: A green test that never sees rejection
Communication goal: Explain the limits of a success-only test double and distinguish a failure-path unit test from real-adapter integration coverage.
Language guidance: A double that always succeeds can support a success-path assertion, but it cannot exercise rejection handling. Configure a failing response and check the user-visible consequence relevant to the known bug.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The storage double always succeeds. / This test exercises the happy path. / The bug showed success after a rejected save. / The current test never triggers rejection. / Make the double reject in a separate case. / Assert that no success message appears after rejection. / Keep the successful-save case too. / A storage call alone does not prove the right user message. / This is a double, not the real adapter. / The result applies to the simulated rejection case. / The real adapter needs separate integration coverage. / Green tests do not establish all storage behavior. / We have not verified the real adapter. / What observable result should this case check? / Please include the configured outcome and assertion. / The new unit case can close one gap, not every storage risk.
Vocabulary: unit test: Focused test of a small behavior or component. / test double: Substitute for a real dependency during testing. / stub: Test substitute supplying predetermined responses. / mock: Test substitute that can record or check interactions. / fixture: Prepared state or data used by a test. / success response: Result indicating an operation succeeded. / rejection: Failed outcome reported by the storage operation here. / exception: Error signal raised during execution under the relevant model. / assertion: Explicit check of an expected test condition. / negative assertion: Check that an unwanted event or state does not occur. / test isolation: Separation of a tested unit from unrelated dependencies. / integration test: Test of interaction between connected components. / adapter: Component translating between an application and an external interface. / branch coverage: Measure of which decision outcomes a test set executes. / happy path: Expected successful execution path. / error path: Execution path handling an unsuccessful operation. / false negative: Test result that misses a defect which is present. / test oracle: Rule or reference used to judge the expected result. / arrange: Prepare the conditions for a test. / act: Execute the behavior being tested. / assert: Check the observed result against the expectation. / interaction verification: Check of how a component called its dependencies. / behavioral verification: Check of the externally observable result. / coverage gap: Relevant behavior not exercised by the current tests.
Speaking task: Read the save-test exchange, stressing always succeeds when describing the original double. Swap roles and repeat turns 5-12. Distinguish a no-success-message assertion from checking a dependency call. Use the final four turns to rehearse a bounded test report. Include the simulated rejection and remaining real-adapter gap.
Partner: Owen: Developer maintaining the test / Farah: Developer reviewing coverage
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the review note about the current test and the proposed rejection case.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Owen: After adding rejection, I'll report exactly which outcome was simulated and which visible behavior was checked, alongside the real-adapter gap.
Farah: That is useful behavioral verification. Report the assertion result, not a blanket statement that storage is fully tested.
Owen: The patch is still pending review. I'll attach both test results when they're available, without inventing a successful run in the handoff.
Farah: Good. A targeted unit test can catch this application defect while leaving the real integration question clearly open.
END REFERENCE
06Comparing performance tradeoffs fairlyFifteen milliseconds faster, forty megabytes over budget+

Your goal

Compare measured latency and memory under matched conditions while separating median improvement from budget compliance and production claims.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Fifteen milliseconds faster, forty megabytes over budget

In a controlled benchmark using the same input and machine, implementation A has median latency of 40 milliseconds and memory use of 80 megabytes. B has median latency of 25 milliseconds and memory use of 160 megabytes. The memory budget is 120 megabytes. Developers Sora and Malik discuss the tradeoff. Tail latency and production behavior have not been measured. B reduces the measured median by 15 milliseconds, or 37.5 percent relative to A, but exceeds the memory budget by 40 megabytes.

Sora: Developer presenting the benchmark / Malik: Developer reviewing the tradeoff

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which conditions are matched?

Answer and explanation

B. Input and machine

The benchmark explicitly uses the same input and machine for both implementations.

2. How far is B over the memory budget?

Answer and explanation

C. 40 megabytes

B uses 160 megabytes against a budget of 120, exceeding it by forty.

3. What is unmeasured?

Answer and explanation

B. Tail latency and production behavior

Only the benchmark median and memory figures are supplied, not tail or production results.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

B reduces the median from 40 to 25 milliseconds: a 15-millisecond difference and a 37.5 percent reduction relative to A. It uses twice A's measured memory, but doubling memory is not the same as doubling speed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
benchmark
Structured measurement under specified conditions.run a benchmark
latency
Time taken for a request or operation.measure latency
median
Middle ordered value, or mean of the two middle values for an even sample.report median latency
tail latency
Slower-end behavior in a latency distribution.measure tail latency
percentile
Value below which a stated percentage of observations falls.report a latency percentile
p95
Ninety-fifth percentile of a measured distribution.distinguish p95 from the median
throughput
Amount of work completed per unit of time.measure throughput separately
memory footprint
Amount of memory used under the stated conditions.compare the memory footprint
resource budget
Allowed limit for a specified resource.respect the resource budget
headroom
Remaining capacity below a stated limit.calculate memory headroom
overrun
Amount by which usage exceeds a limit.quantify the budget overrun
baseline
Reference implementation or measurement for comparison.use A as the baseline
relative reduction
Decrease expressed as a fraction of the original value.calculate the relative reduction
absolute difference
Direct numerical subtraction between comparable measurements.report the absolute difference
tradeoff
Gain in one property accompanied by a cost in another.explain the tradeoff
workload
Type and amount of work applied to a system.describe the workload
sample
Set of observations used in an analysis.document the sample
distribution
Pattern of observed values across a range.inspect the latency distribution
outlier
Observation unusually distant from most other values.investigate an outlier
measurement noise
Variation not attributable to the intended comparison alone.account for measurement noise
controlled conditions
Specified conditions held consistent during comparison.preserve controlled conditions
production behavior
Behavior under actual live operating conditions.avoid assuming production behavior
bottleneck
Constraint limiting overall system performance.identify the bottleneck
budget compliance
Whether measured usage satisfies the stated limit.check budget compliance

A uses 80 megabytes, leaving 40 below the 120-megabyte budget. B uses 160, exceeding it by 40. Neither median describes the unmeasured tail, and neither controlled result establishes production behavior.

C Notice the language

Do not hide a constraint inside a headline

Faster is incomplete when a resource budget matters. State the metric, conditions, comparison baseline, and constraint together. A colleague can acknowledge a genuine latency improvement while rejecting the claim that it settles the overall implementation decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the conditions
Both runs use the same input and machine.
State A
A has a forty-millisecond median and uses eighty megabytes.
State B
B has a twenty-five-millisecond median and uses one hundred sixty megabytes.
Report the absolute gain
B lowers the median by fifteen milliseconds.
Name the baseline
That is a thirty-seven-point-five percent reduction relative to A.
Report the cost
B uses twice A's measured memory.
State the constraint
The memory budget is one hundred twenty megabytes.
Calculate the overrun
B exceeds that budget by forty megabytes.
Calculate the headroom
A remains forty megabytes below the limit.
Avoid a vague verdict
B is faster on this median measure, not automatically better overall.
Limit the metric
The median does not tell us the tail latency.
Name the missing evidence
We have not measured production behavior.
Separate throughput
These figures do not establish throughput.
Request a balanced summary
Put the latency gain and memory cost together.
Keep the decision bounded
The measured gain does not remove the budget constraint.
Close without overclaiming
We have a documented tradeoff, not a production guarantee.
6 language and precision notes
Relative to A
Names the denominator for the percentage comparison.
By versus to
By gives the change; to gives the resulting value.
Twice
Compares B's memory with A's, not with the budget.
On this measure
Limits a faster claim to the reported metric.
Does not tell us
Prevents inference from median to unmeasured tail behavior.
Not automatically
Preserves the benefit while resisting an overall conclusion.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What is B's median reduction relative to A?

Answer and explanation

B. 37.5 percent

Fifteen divided by the forty-millisecond baseline equals 0.375, or 37.5 percent.

2. Which budget statement is correct?

Answer and explanation

C. A is 40 below and B is 40 above

The 120-megabyte limit lies forty above A and forty below B.

3. Which claim exceeds the evidence?

Answer and explanation

D. B improves every production request

Production behavior and every-request outcomes have not been measured.

4. Which summary is balanced?

Answer and explanation

D. B lowers median latency but exceeds the memory budget; tail and production remain unmeasured

The summary combines the measured benefit, resource constraint, and remaining evidence limits.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Fifteen milliseconds faster, forty megabytes over budget

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sora: Developer presenting the benchmark / Malik: Developer reviewing the tradeoff

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • relative reduction
  • tradeoff
  • budget compliance
  • benchmark
  • controlled conditions
  • memory footprint
  • Production behavior
  • absolute difference
  • Tail latency
  • headroom
  1. Sora
    B's measured median is twenty-five milliseconds versus A's forty on the same input and machine. I'd like to propose B, but its memory figure complicates that.
  2. Malik
    That is useful 1 evidence. Put the memory figures beside it before we call B the better option. The resource budget is part of this decision.
  3. Sora
    A uses eighty megabytes; B uses a hundred sixty. The budget is a hundred twenty, so B is forty over even in this measured case.
  4. Malik
    Right. Its 2 is twice A's and forty megabytes over budget. The latency improvement does not cancel that constraint.
  5. Sora
    For the percentage, I subtracted twenty-five from forty and divided the fifteen-millisecond decrease by forty. That gives thirty-seven-point-five percent.
  6. Malik
    Yes, that is the 3 relative to A. Keep the baseline explicit so nobody divides by twenty-five and reports a different quantity under the same label.
  7. Sora
    Would faster be too loose in the headline? I measured latency, not how many requests per second either version handles.
  8. Malik
    Prefer the precise 4 and percentage latency reduction. Those statements tell readers exactly which measurement changed without suggesting an unmeasured throughput result.
  9. Sora
    We didn't collect tail percentiles. A lower median could coexist with worse slow requests, so I can't claim improvement for every request.
  10. Malik
    Correct. 5 remains unmeasured. Do not turn a better middle value into a claim about every request or a specific percentile we did not record.
  11. Sora
    A is forty megabytes below the same budget. I'll keep that separate from B's forty-megabyte excess rather than call both numbers spare memory.
  12. Malik
    That is forty megabytes of 6 under the stated budget. It is a different claim from saying A has more memory available under all possible workloads.
  13. Sora
    These are benchmark runs, not live measurements. Should I put that boundary alongside the numbers instead of burying it in a note?
  14. Malik
    Yes. 7 is not established by these figures. Name that limit instead of relying on readers to infer it from the word benchmark.
  15. Sora
    Then the proposal needs both sides: fifteen milliseconds lower median, twice the memory, and B above the present limit.
  16. Malik
    That presents the 8 fairly. It acknowledges a real improvement without treating one favorable number as a complete engineering decision.
  17. Sora
    I could request a changed memory budget, but that wouldn't make this run compliant with the current one. It would be a separate decision.
  18. Malik
    Exactly. Current 9 is clear: A is below the limit and B is above it. A hypothetical future budget should not rewrite today's comparison.
  19. Sora
    I'll report the matched conditions, latency decrease, memory figures, and unmeasured tail and live behavior. We still need to decide what to investigate next.
  20. Malik
    Good. Keep the 10 visible alongside that summary. We can make a reasoned next decision without pretending the benchmark answers questions it never measured.

Answers and explanations
  1. benchmark Turn 2. Benchmark identifies the controlled measurement, whose conditions limit the scope of the comparison.
  2. memory footprint Turn 4. Memory footprint is the measured memory usage, which is 160 megabytes for B against a 120-megabyte budget.
  3. relative reduction Turn 6. Relative reduction divides the fifteen-millisecond decrease by A's forty-millisecond baseline, yielding 37.5 percent.
  4. absolute difference Turn 8. Absolute difference is the direct fifteen-millisecond reduction, which can be reported alongside the relative latency reduction.
  5. Tail latency Turn 10. Tail latency concerns slower-end observations and cannot be inferred from the supplied median alone.
  6. headroom Turn 12. Headroom is the forty-megabyte difference between A's eighty-megabyte usage and the 120-megabyte limit.
  7. Production behavior Turn 14. Production behavior remains unmeasured, so the controlled benchmark cannot establish live-system outcomes.
  8. tradeoff Turn 16. Tradeoff connects B's lower median latency with its higher memory use and failure to meet the stated budget.
  9. budget compliance Turn 18. Budget compliance is evaluated against the stated 120-megabyte limit, which B exceeds regardless of its latency gain.
  10. controlled conditions Turn 20. Controlled conditions specify the matched input and machine and prevent the result being generalized without further evidence.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Faster at the median, outside the memory budget

Fictional developers compare implementations A and B under the supplied benchmark.

  1. Developer: B's median latency is twenty-five milliseconds, versus forty for A. Should we choose B?
  2. Reviewer: How does each memory footprint compare with our stated budget?
  3. Developer: A uses eighty megabytes; B uses one hundred sixty. Our budget is one hundred twenty.
  4. Reviewer: Then B improves that latency measure but exceeds the resource constraint we're working within.
  5. Developer: Agreed. A fits the memory budget, though its median is fifteen milliseconds slower.
  6. Reviewer: Do these results tell us about the slowest requests or only the median?
  7. Developer: Only the median. We haven't measured tail latency or representative production workloads yet.
  8. Reviewer: Please include those limits instead of describing B as universally faster and therefore better.
  9. Developer: I'll present A as within budget and B as requiring a memory tradeoff we haven't accepted.
  10. Reviewer: That comparison gives us a decision constraint, not just the most attractive single number.
Throughput rises while individual waits grow

Fictional developers interpret two different measures in a batching experiment.

  1. Developer: The batched version completes more items per minute, but individual items wait longer before processing.
  2. Colleague: Then improved throughput doesn't necessarily mean each person gets a faster response, does it?
  3. Developer: Correct. Throughput measures completed work over time; latency measures the wait for a specified item.
  4. Colleague: Which goal matters for this feature? The current requirement is a response within the stated interaction window.
  5. Developer: Then we need the latency evidence against that window, not only the items-per-minute total.
  6. Colleague: Can we show both metrics for the same workload so the tradeoff is visible?
  7. Developer: Yes. The benchmark includes both, although we haven't measured the busiest production pattern.
  8. Colleague: Please keep that limitation. A larger batch might help total capacity while making this interaction less responsive.
  9. Developer: I'll explain the measured tradeoff without presenting higher throughput as a synonym for lower latency.
  10. Colleague: And we'll ask whether the observed wait meets the actual requirement before favoring the batch.
A warm run beside a cold run

Fictional developers correct an unfair benchmark comparison.

  1. Reviewer: Your chart compares A's first run with B's fifth run. Were their starting conditions equivalent?
  2. Developer: No. B reused initialized data, while A's first run included preparing that data.
  3. Reviewer: Then we can't attribute the entire timing difference to the implementation change alone.
  4. Developer: Agreed. The warm and cold conditions need separate comparisons using matching workloads.
  5. Reviewer: Can you label the existing results honestly instead of discarding the slower observation?
  6. Developer: Yes. I'll mark them cold A and warm B, without claiming a controlled head-to-head result.
  7. Reviewer: What comparison would answer whether B changes the first-use experience for this workload?
  8. Developer: Cold runs for both implementations under the same stated preparation conditions would address that question.
  9. Reviewer: And warm runs for both would answer the repeated-use question, which is different.
  10. Developer: Exactly. I'll keep the two questions separate so a cache advantage doesn't become an unsupported implementation claim.
